Existence of minimizers for a two-phase free boundary problem with coherent and incoherent interfaces MUR Excellence Department 2023-2027
Randy Llerena, Paolo Piovano

Abstract
. A variational model for describing the morphology of two-phase continua by allowing for the interplay between coherent and incoherent interfaces is introduced. Coherent interfaces are characterized by the microscopical arrangement of atoms of the two materials in a homogeneous lattice, with deformation being the solely stress relief mechanism, while at incoherent interfaces delamination between the two materials occurs. The model is designed in the framework of the theory of Stress Driven Rearrangement Instabilities, which are characterized by the competition between elastic and surface effects. The existence of energy minimizers is established in the plane by means of the direct method of the calculus of variations under a constraint on the number of boundary connected components of the underlying phase, whose exterior boundary is prescribed to satify a graph assumption, and of the two-phase composite region. Both the wetting and the dewetting regimes are included in the analysis.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
49J10, 49Q15, 49Q20, 35R35 , 74A45, 74G65

1.
Introduction

In this manuscript we address the problem of providing a mathematical variational framework for the description of the morphology and the elastic properties of two-phase continua based on Gibbs’s notion of a sharp phase-interface dividing them
[
13
,
31
,
35
]
. In the presence of two interacting media large stresses due to the different crystalline order of the two materials originate and, besides bulk deformation, various types of morphological destabilization may occur as a further strain relief mode. These are often referred to as the family
of
stress driven rearrangement instabilities
(SDRI)
[
5
,
20
,
34
,
36
,
49
]
, which include the roughness of the exposed crystalline boundaries, the formation of cracks in the bulk materials, the nucleation of dislocations in the crystalline lattices, and the delamination (as opposed to the adhesion) at the contact regions with the other material.

Literature provides extensive studies of these phenomena under the assumption that one phase is a rigid fixed continuous medium underlying, such as the substrates for epitaxially-strained thin films
[
14
,
17
,
27
,
39
]
, or constraining, such as crystal cavities
[
26
]
or the containers in capillarity problems
[
23
]
, the other phase, which is instead let free, or by modeling the interactions with other media simply by means of fixed boundary conditions. There are though settings in which the hierarchy between the phases is not clear, or a rigidity ranking between them is not easily identifiable, since the interplay among the deformation and the interface instabilities affecting all phases is crucial, such as, in the shock-induced transformations and mechanical twinning
[
35
]
or in the deposition of film multilayers
[
42
]
.

As described in
[
35
]
the extension of classical theories of continuum mechanics to two-phase deformable media is though “not as straightforward as it might appear”, since combining the accretion and deletion of material constituents responsible for the moving of the interface between the two phases and their boundaries, with the framework of elasticity related to bulk deformation and fractures
[
15
,
30
]
, by quoting
[
35
]
, “leads to conceptual difficulties”. A critical modeling issue related to the interface between the two phases is the interplay between
coherency
, that is here intended as the microscopical arrangement of atoms of the two materials in a homogeneous lattice, with deformation being the solely stress relief mechanism
[
35
]
, and
incoherency
, that instead refers to the debonding occurring between the atoms of the two materials
[
13
]
, which results in the composite delamination at the two-phase interface
[
40
]
.
This manuscript seems to be, to the best of our knowledge, the first attempt to provide a mathematical framework able to simultaneously describe coherent and incoherent interfaces for a two-phase setting, which we carry out by also keeping in the picture the other features of SDRI, such as the dichotomy between the
wetting regime
,
that is the setting in which it is more
convenient for a phase to cover the surface of the other phase with an infinitesimal layer of atoms,
and the
dewetting regime
, in which it is preferable to let such surface exposed to the vapor.

The studies for the setting of only coherent interfaces go back to Almgren
[
1
]
, who was the first to formulate the problem in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d>1
, in the context without elasticity for surface tensions proportional among the various interfaces, by means of
integral currents
in geometric measure theory and by singling out a condition ensuring the lower semicontinuity of the overall surface energy with respect to the
L^{1}
-convergence of the sets in the partition. Then, Ambrosio and Braides in
[
2
,
3
]
extended the setting to also non-proportional surface tensions by introducing a new integral condition referred to as
BV
-
ellipticity
, which they show to be both sufficient and necessary for the lower semicontinuity with respect to the
L^{1}
-convergence. As such condition is the analogous, for the setting of
Caccioppoli partitions
, of Morrey’s
quasi-convexity
, it is difficult to check it in practice. In
[
2
,
3
]

BV
-ellipticity is proved to coincide with a simpler to check

triangle inequality condition
among surface tensions for the case of partitions in 3 sets (like the setting considered in this paper, in which one element of the partition is always represented by the
vapor
outside the two phases), which was then confirmed to be the only case by
[
10
]
. Other conditions therefore have been introduced, such as
B
-
convexity
and
joint convexity
, with though
BV
-ellipticity remaining so far the only condition known to be both necessary and sufficient for lower semicontinuity apart from specific settings (see
[
11
,
45
]
for more details). Recently, the
BV
-ellipticity has been extended in the context of
BD
-
spaces
in
[
32
]
. Finally, we refer to
[
7
]
for a variant of the
Ohta–Kawasaki model
considered to model thin films of diblock copolymers in the unconfined case, which represents a recent example in the literature of a two-phase model in the absence of elasticity and of incoherent and crack interfaces, under a graph constraint for union of the two phases.

Regarding incoherent interfaces the problem is intrinsically related to the renowned
segmentation problem
in image reconstruction that was actually originally introduced by Mumford and Shah in
[
46
]
with a multiphase formulation, as a partition problem of an original image, with the connceted contours of the image areas characterized as discontinuity set of an auxiliary state function.
Then, the approaches developed to tackle the problem led to the study of a single phase setting with the jump set of the state function representing internal interfaces, proven to satisfy
Ahlfors-type regularity
result
[
4
,
19
]
. Such single-phase framework has been then extended to the context of linear elasticity in fracture
mechanics with the state function being vectorial and representing the bulk displacement of a crystalline material and the energy replaced by the
Griffith energy

[
15
,
30
]
. The attempt to recover the original setting of
[
46
]
in a rigorous mathematical formulation (apart from some formulations with piecewise-constant state functions or numerical investigations) has been then addressed by Bucur, Fragalà, and Giacomini in
[
8
]
and
[
9
]
(see also
[
16
]
for a related multiphase boundary problem in the context of reaction-diffusion systems). In
[
8
]
Ahlfors-type regularity is established for
ad hoc
notions of multiphase local
almost-quasi minimizers
of an energy accounting for incoherent isotropic interfaces and disregarding the contribution of the coherent portions, while in
[
9
]
they introduce a multiphase version of the Mumford-Shah problem by treating all the reduced phase boundaries as incoherent interfaces (like the internal jump sets) and by adding an extra (statistical) term, which induces multi-phase minimizers.

In order to finally include in the model both coherent and incoherent portions (possibly also on the same interface between the phases), we first restrict to the two-phase setting (for a multi-phase setting in the context of film multilayers we refer to the related paper
[
42
]
under finalization) and
follow a different direction than the one of
[
8
,
9
]
, which works for
d=2
: We adopt the approach considered in
[
36
,
37
]
, that was relying on the strategy developed for the Mumford-Shah problem in
[
19
]
. Such approach consists in first imposing a fixed constraint on the number of connected components for the boundary of the free phases, in order then to employ adaptations of Golab’s Theorem
[
33
]
for proving the compactness with respect to a proper selected topology, and then in studying the convergence of the solutions of the different minimum problems related to different constraints on the connected components, as such constraints tend to infinity. This second step has been performed for the one-phase setting in
[
37
]
(and for higher dimension in
[
38
]
) by means of density estimates.

Here, we performed the first step in this program, reaching an existence result analogous to the one in
[
36
]
.
However, the extension of
[
36
]
to the two-phase setting requires important modification in the model setting, since characterizing the incoherent interface as the jump portion of the bulk displacement on the two-phase interface as in
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
appears to be not feasible, as in our setting the two-phase interfaces need to be considered much less regular
than Lipschitz manifolds like in
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
. To solve this issue we consider as set variables of the energy not the two phases, to which we refer to as the
film
and the
substrate phase
, but the substrate phase and the whole region occupied by the composite of both the two phases, and we characterize the incoherent interfaces as a portion of the boundary intersection of such variables. As a byproduct of this strategy we do not need to impose a constraint on the number of boundary components of the film phases (but only with respect to the substrates and the composite regions), so that the physical relevant setting of countable separated isolated film islands forming on top of the substrate is included in our analysis, even though it was prevented by the formulation in
[
36
]
. Moreover, we can also extend
[
36
]
to the presence of adjacent materials for the Griffith-type model with mismatch strain and delamination
[
15
,
30
,
38
]
.

In agreement with the SDRI theory
[
5
,
20
,
34
]
the total energy
\mathcal{F}
is given by the sum of two contributions, namely the elastic energy
\mathcal{W}
and the surface energy
\mathcal{S}
, and it is defined on triples
(A,S,u)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, where
u
represent the
bulk displacement
of the composite material of the two phases, and
A
and
S
are sets whose closures represents the
composite region
and the
substrate region
, respectively, while the
film region
is given by
\overline{A}\setminus S^{(1)}
(for
S^{(1)}
denoting the points with density 1 in
S
). More precisely, given
\Omega:=(-l,l)\times_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(-L,L)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
as the region where the composite material is located, which is defined for the two parameters
l,L>0
and to which we refer as the
container
in analogy to the notation of capillarity problems, we introduce

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}:=\Large\{(A,S,u):\hskip 14.40004pt

A
and
S
are
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-measurable sets with
S\subset\overline{A}\subset\overline{\Omega}
such that

\partial A\cap{\rm{Int}}(S)=\emptyset
,
\partial A
and
\partial S
are
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable,

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial A)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S)<\infty$, and
$u\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{{loc}}}({\rm{Int}}(A);\mathbb{R}^{2})$}\}.

We define
\mathcal{F}:\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

\mathcal{F}(A,S,u):=\mathcal{S}(A,S)+\mathcal{W}(A,u)

for every
(A,S,u)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
.
The elastic energy
\mathcal{W}(A,u)
is defined analogously to
[
21
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
by

\mathcal{W}(A,u):=\int_{A}{W\left(x,E\left(u\left(x\right)-E_{0}\left(x\right)\right)\right)dx},

where the elastic density
W
is determined by the quadratic form

W\left(x,M\right):=\mathbb{C}\left(x\right)M:M,

for a fourth-order tensor
\mathbb{C}:\Omega\to\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\rm sym}
,
E
denotes the symmetric gradient, i.e.,
E(v):=\frac{\nabla v+\nabla^{T}v}{2}
for any
v\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{{loc}}}({\rm{Int}}(A);\mathbb{R}^{2})
, representing the
strain
, and
E_{0}
is
the
mismatch strain

x\in\Omega\mapsto E_{0}\left(x\right)\in\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\mathrm{{sym}}}
defined as

E_{0}:=\begin{cases}E(u_{0})&\text{in }\Omega\setminus S,\\
0&\text{in }S,\end{cases}

for a fixed
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The mismatch strain is included in the SDRI theory to represent the fact that the two phases are given by possibly different crystalline materials whose free-standing equilibrium lattice could present a lattice mismatch. In this context notice that
\mathbb{C}
is allowed to present discontinuities at the interface between the two materials (see hypothesis (H3) in Section
3.2
).

The surface energy
\mathcal{S}(A,S)
is given by

\mathcal{S}(A,S):=\int_{\Omega\cap(\partial A\cup\partial S)}{\psi(x,\nu(x))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)},

where, by denoting with
\nu_{U}(z)
the normal unit vector pointing outward to a set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable boundary at a point
x\in\partial U
,

\nu(x):=\begin{cases}\nu_{A}(x)&\text{if $z\in\partial A\setminus\partial S$},\\
\nu_{S}(x)&\text{if $z\in\partial S$,}\end{cases}

and
\psi:\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,\infty]
represents the
surface tension of the composite
of the two phases, which we allow to be anisotropic.

In order to properly define
\psi
we need to consider the three surface tensions
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}},\,\varphi_{\mathrm{S}},\,\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}:\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,\infty]
characterizing the three possible interfaces for the two-phase setting, i.e., the interface between the film phase and the vapor, the interface between the substrate phase and the vapor, and the interface between the film and the substrate phases.
Furthermore, to simultaneously treat both the wetting and the dewetting regime, we introduce two auxiliary surface tensions, to which we refer as the
regime surface tensions
, that we defined as:

\varphi:=\min\{\varphi_{\mathrm{S}},\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi^{\prime}:=\min\{\varphi_{\mathrm{S}},\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}\},

since
\varphi_{\mathrm{S}}
is the surface tension associated to the dewetting regime, as the substrate surface remains exposed to the vapor, while
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}
and
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}
are both associated to the wetting regime, respectively, to the situation of an infinitesimal layer of film atoms covering the substrate surface (by being bonded to the substrate atoms), which is referred to as the
wetting layer
, or of simply a detached
film filament
.
We define

(1.1)

\psi(x,\nu(x)):=\begin{cases}\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap(\partial^{*}A\setminus\partial^{*}{S}),\\
\varphi(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap\partial^{*}{S}\cap\partial^{*}A,\\
\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap(\partial^{*}{S}\setminus\partial A),\\
(\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}+\varphi)(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap\partial^{*}{S}\cap\partial A\cap A^{(1)},\\
2\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap\partial A\cap A^{(1)}\cap S^{(0)},\\
2\varphi^{\prime}(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap\partial A\cap A^{(0)},\\
2\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap(\partial{S}\setminus\partial A)\cap\left(S^{(1)}\cup S^{(0)}\right)\cap A^{(1)},\\
2\varphi(x,\nu\left(x\right))&x\in\Omega\cap\partial{S}\cap\partial A\cap S^{(1)},\end{cases}

where
\partial^{*}U
and
U^{(\alpha)}
denote, when well defined, the
reduced boundary
and the set of points of density
\alpha\in[0,1]
for a set
U\subset\mathcal{R}^{2}
. We notice that the 8 subregions of the domain
\Omega\cap(\partial A\cup\partial S)
in which the definition of
\psi
is distinguished are the counterpart of the 5 terms appearing in the surface energy of

[
36
]
for the two-phase setting (see Remark
3.13
for more details). Each subregion appearing in (
1.1
) represents, by moving line by line, the
film free boundary
, the
substrate free boundary
, the
film-substrate coherent interface
, the
film-substrate incoherent interface
, the
film cracks
, the
exposed filaments
,
the
substrate filaments and cracks
in the film-substrate coherent interface, and the
substrate cracks
in the film-substrate incoherent interface, respectively.

We observe that the surface tensions associated to the film free boundary, the coherent substrate-film interface, and the substrate free boundary, are simply
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}
,
\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}
, and (to accommodate both wetting and dewetting regimes)
\varphi
respectively, while the surface tension associated to the incoherent film-substrate interface is chosen to be
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}+\varphi
in analogy with the film-substrate delamination or delaminated region in
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
, since the incoherent interface coincides with the portion of the film-substrate interface in which there is no bonding between the film and the substrate surfaces. All remaining 4 terms are weighted double (in analogy to the lower-semicontinuity results previously obtained in
[
14
,
21
,
27
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
for the one-phase setting) as they refer to either material filaments in the void or cracks in the composite bulk. In particular, we notice that in the substrate bulk region represented by
S^{(1)}
we distinguish between substrate cracks in the coherent and in the incoherent film-substrate interface, that are counted with weight
2\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}
and
2\varphi
, respectively, while in the film bulk region
A^{(1)}\cap S^{(0)}
we distinguish between substrate filaments that are not film cracks counted with
2\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}
and film cracks counted
2\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}
(see Figure
1
).

Figure 1
.
The admissible regions for an admissible configuration
(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{m}}
(see Definition
3.2
) are represented by indicating the substrate region
\overline{S}
and the film region
\overline{A}\setminus S^{(1)}

with a darker and a lighter gray, respectively. In particular, the film and the substrate free boundaries (with the film and substrate filaments) are indicated with a thinner line, while the film-substrate interface is depicted with a thicker line that is continuous or dashed to distinguish between its incoherent portions and its coherent portions (inclusive of substrate cracks and filaments that are not film cracks), respectively.

The main result of the paper consists in finding a physically relevant family of admissible configurations in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, which is denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{m}}
for
\textbf{m}:=(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, in which we can prove that, under a
two-phase volume constraint
,
\mathcal{F}
admits a minimizer. We find such a family
\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{m}}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
by considering as admissible configurations
(A,S,u)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
the ones for which (see Definition
3.2
for more details):

-

the number of boundary connected components of
S
and
A
are fixed to be at most
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
, respectively,

-

the substrate regions
S
satisfy an
exterior graph constraint
consisting in requiring that
\partial^{*}S\cup(\partial S\cap S^{(0)})
is the graph of an upper semicontinuous function with pointwise bounded variation (while internal, also non-graph-like, substrate cracks are allowed),

as shown in Figure
1
. We notice that such an exterior graph constraint allows to have a more involved description of the substrate regions than the previously considered graph constraint in the literature for the one-phase setting
[
14
,
17
,
21
,
27
]
, which is indeed needed to achieve the compactness result contained in Theorem
3.11
.

Therefore, for any two volume parameters
\mathbbm{v}_{0},\mathbbm{v}_{1}\in[\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega)/2,\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega)]
such that
\mathbbm{v}_{0}\leq\mathbbm{v}_{1}
, we consider the problem:

(1.2)

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{l}\hskip 16.38895pt(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{m}}\\
\mathcal{L}^{2}(S)=\mathbbm{v}_{0},\,\mathcal{L}^{2}({A})=\mathbbm{v}_{1}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{F}(A,S,u)},

which we tackle by employing the
direct method
of the calculus of variations, namely by equipping
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
with a properly chosen topology
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
sufficiently weak to establish a compactness property for energy-equibounded sequences in
\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{m}}
and strong enough to prove the lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{m}}
.
The topology
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
is characterized by the convergence:

(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{\tau_{\mathcal{C}}}(A,S,u)\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\begin{cases}\text{$\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(\partial A_{k}\right)}<\infty$, $\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(\partial S_{k}\right)}<\infty$,}\\
\text{$\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial A_{k}\right)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial A\right)$ locally uniformly in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$},\\
\text{$\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial S_{k}\right)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial S\right)$ locally uniformly in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$,}\\
u_{k}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}u\quad\text{a.e.\ in $\mathrm{{Int}\left(A\right)}$,}\end{cases}

where the
signed distance function
is defined for any
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
as follows

{\rm{sdist}}(x,\partial E):=\begin{cases}{\rm dist}(x,E)&\text{if }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E,\\
-{\rm dist}(x,E)&\text{if }x\in E.\end{cases}

The compactness property shared by energy-equibounded sequences
(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
that we establish in Theorem
3.11
consists in the existence, up to a subsequence, of a possibly different sequence
(\widetilde{A}_{k},\widetilde{S_{k}},\widetilde{u_{k}})\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
compact in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
with respect to
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
such that

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{F}(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})}=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{F}\left(\widetilde{A}_{k},\widetilde{S_{k}},\widetilde{u_{k}}\right)}.

This is achieved by both extending to the two-phase setting and to the situation with the exterior graph constraint the strategy used in
[
36
, Theorem 2.7]
. For the latter, we rely on the arguments already used in
[
14
,
27
]
, while for the former we used the Blaschke-type selection principle proved in
[
36
, Proposition 3.1]
together with the Golab’s Theorem
[
33
, Theorem 2.1]
and we implement to the two-phase setting the construction of
[
36
, Proposition 3.6]
. Such construction is needed to take care of those connected components of
A_{k}
that separate in the limit in multiple connected components, e.g., in the case of
neckpinches, in order to properly apply
Korn’s inequality
just after having introduced extra boundary to create different components also at the level
A_{k}
(by passing to the sequence with composite regions
\widetilde{A}_{k}
). We notice though that the characterization of the delamination region introduced in this manuscript allows for a simplification in the arguments used
[
36
, Theorem 2.7]
, as the surface energy does not involve the bulk displacements, also yielding an extension of the result by including the situation with
S\neq\emptyset
and
\mathbbm{v}_{1}:=\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega)
.

The crucial point in proving the
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
-lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{F}
is the
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
-lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{S}
, as
the
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
-lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{W}
directly follows by convexity similarly to
[
27
,
36
]
. In order to establish the
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
-lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{S}
in Proposition
5.13
we fix
(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
and
(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
such that

(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{C}}}(A,S,u)
, and we associate the positive Radon measures
\mu_{k}
and
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to the localized energy versions of
\mathcal{S}(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})
and
\mathcal{S}(A,S,u)
, respectively. We have that

(1.3)

\liminf\limits_{k\to+\infty}\mathcal{S}(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})\geq\mathcal{S}(A,S,u)\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\liminf\limits_{k\to+\infty}\mu_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\geq\mu(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

and since, up to a subsequence,
\mu_{k}
weakly* converges to some positive Radon measure
\mu_{0}
, and
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}((\partial A\cup\partial S)\cap\Omega)
, by proving the following estimate involving
Radon-Nikodym derivatives
:

(1.4)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{0}}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Omega\cap(\partial A\cup\partial S))}\geq\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Omega\cap(\partial A\cup\partial S))}\quad\text{$\mathcal{H}^{1}$ -a.e. on $\Omega\cap(\partial A\cup\partial S),$}

which implies that

\lim\mu_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\mu_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\geq\mu(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

in view of (
1.3
), the
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
-lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{S}
follows.

The proof of (
1.4
) is very involved and it is performed by separating
\Omega\cap(\partial A\cup\partial S)
in 12 portions on which we apply a
blow-up technique
(see, e.g.,
[
29
]
) together with
ad hoc
(apart from the 2 portions in which it turns out that we can use
[
43
, Theorem 20.1]
) results, i.e., Lemmas

5.7
-
5.12
, which can be seen as the counterpart in the two-phase setting of
[
36
, Lemmas 4.4 and 4.5]
(see Table
1
for more details on the 12 blow-ups).
In order to prove Lemmas
5.7
-
5.12
, firstly we formalize the notions of
film islands
,
composite voids
, and
substrate grains
(see Definition
5.6
), secondly we prove in Lemma
5.5
that the coherent interface associated to any configuration
(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{m}}
can be regarded, up to an error and a modification of
(A,S,u)
by passing to the family
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, as given by a finite number (depending on the initial configuration
(A,S,u)
) of connected components, and finally we design induction arguments (with respect to the number of such components) in which we are able to use the induction hypothesis by “shrinking” islands, “filling” voids, and “modifying grains in new voids” as depicted in Figures
3
,
4
, and
6
, respectively, by means of employing the
anisotropic minimality of segments

[
43
, Remark 20.3]
.

The manuscript is organized as follows: in Section
2
we specify the notation used throughout this paper, in Section
3
we introduce the model under consideration, present some preliminary results and state the main results, i.e., the existence of a solution to (
1.2
) in Theorem
3.10
together with the compactness result of Theorem
3.11
and the lower semicontinuity result of Theorem
3.12
, in Section
4
, we prove Theorem
3.11
, in Section
5
we prove Theorem
3.12
, and finally in Section
6
we prove Theorem
3.10
.

2.
Notation

In this section we collect the relevant notation used throughout the paper by separating it for different mathematical areas.

Linear algebra

We consider the orthonormal basis
\left\{\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{2}}\right\}=\left\{(1,0),(0,1)\right\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and indicate the coordinates of points
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by
(x_{1},x_{2})
. We indicate by
a\cdot b:=\sum_{i=1}^{2}a_{i}b_{i}
the Euclidean scalar product between points
a
and
b
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and we denote the corresponding norm by
|a|:=\sqrt{a\cdot a}
.

Let
\mathbb{M}^{2}
be the set of
(2\times 2)
-matrices and by
\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\mathrm{sym}}
the space of symmetric
(2\times 2)
-matrices. The space
\mathbb{M}^{2}
is endowed with Frobenius inner product
E:F:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}E_{ij}F_{ij}
and, with a slight abuse of notation, we denote the corresponding norm by
|E|:=\sqrt{E:E}
.

Topology

Since the model considered in this manuscript is two-dimensional, if not otherwise stated, all the sets are contained in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We denote the cartesian product in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of two sets
A\subset\mathbb{R}
and
B\subset\mathbb{R}
by
A\times_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B:=\{(a,b):\,a\in E\text{ and }b\in F\}
. For any set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we denote by
{\rm{Int}}(E)
,
\overline{E}
and
\partial E
interior, the closure and the topological boundary of
E
, respectively. Furthermore, we denote by
Cl(F)
the closure of a set
F
in
E
with respect to the relative topology in
E
. For any
r\in\mathbb{R}
we define
rE:=\left\{rx:x\in E\right\}
.

Given
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
Q_{\rho,\nu}\left(x\right)
is the open square of sidelength
2\rho>0
centered at
x
and whose sides are either perpendicular or parallel to
\nu
. Note that if
\nu=\textbf{e}_{\textbf{1}}=\bm{e}_{\bm{2}}
and
x=0
,
Q_{\rho}:=Q_{\rho,\nu}(0)=\left(-\rho,\rho\right)\times\left(-\rho,\rho\right)
. We define by
I_{\rho}
the symmetric segment
I_{\rho}:=\left[-\rho,\rho\right]\subset\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, a
(parametrized) curve
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a continuous function
r:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
injective in
(a,b)
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
and its image is referred to as the
support of the curve
. Moreover, since any continuous bijection between compact sets has a continuous inverse, the support of any curve is homeomorphic to a closed interval in
\mathbb{R}
(see
[
25
, Section 3.2]
).

Given
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\rho>0
, we define the
blow-up map
centered in
y_{0}
with radius
\rho
as the function
\sigma_{\rho,y_{0}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\sigma_{\rho,y_{0}}\left(y\right):=\frac{y-y_{0}}{\rho}

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Observe that if we apply
\sigma_{\rho,x}
to
Q_{\rho,\nu}\left(x\right)
and
\overline{Q_{\rho,\nu}\left(x\right)}
, we obtain that
\sigma_{\rho,x}\left(Q_{\rho,\nu}\left(x\right)\right)=Q_{1,\nu}(0)
and
\sigma_{\rho,x}(\overline{Q_{\rho,\nu}\left(x\right)})=\overline{Q_{1,\nu}(0)}
. When
y_{0}=0
, we write
\sigma_{\rho}
instead of
\sigma_{\rho,0}
. We denote by
\pi_{i}
the projections onto
x_{i}
-axis for
i=1,2
, i.e., the maps
\pi_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\pi_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
are such that
\pi_{1}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{1}
and
\pi_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{2}
for every
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Finally, let
{\rm dist}(\cdot,E)
and
{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E)
be the
distance
function from
E
and the
signed distance
from
\partial E
respectively, where we recall that
{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E)
is defined by

{\rm{sdist}}(x,\partial E):=\begin{cases}{\rm dist}(x,E)&\textrm{if }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E,\\
-{\rm dist}(x,E)&\textrm{if }x\in E\end{cases}

for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Geometric measure theory

We denote by
\mathcal{L}^{2}{(B)}
the
2
-dimensional Lebesgue measure of any Lebesgue measurable set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and by
\mathbbm{1}_{B}
the characteristic function of
B
. For
\alpha\in\left[0,1\right]
we denote by
B^{(\alpha)}
the set of points of density
\alpha
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, i.e.,

B^{(\alpha)}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\lim_{r\to 0}{\frac{\mathcal{L}^{2}({B\cap B_{r}\left(x\right)})}{\mathcal{L}^{2}({B_{r}\left(x\right)})}}=\alpha\right\}.

We denote the distributional derivative of a function
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by
Df
and define it as the operator
D:C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{Df\cdot\varphi}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{f\cdot\gradient\varphi\,dx},

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

We denote with
\mathcal{H}^{1}
the 1-dimensional Hausdorff measure. We say that
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable if
0<\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)<+\infty
and
\theta_{*}(K,x)=\theta^{*}(K,x)=1
for
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-a.e.
x\in K
, where

\theta_{*}(K,x):=\liminf_{r\to 0^{+}}{\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(K\cap B_{r}\left(x\right))}{2r}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\theta^{*}(K,x):=\limsup_{r\to 0^{+}}{\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(K\cap B_{r}\left(x\right))}{2r}}.

We define sets of finite perimeter as in
[
4
, Definion 3.35]
and the reduced boundary
\partial^{*}E
of a set
E
of finite perimeter by

(2.1)

\partial^{*}E:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\exists\nu_{E}\left(x\right):=-\lim_{r\to 0}{\frac{D\mathbbm{1}_{E}(B_{r}\left(x\right))}{\absolutevalue{D \mathbbm{1}_ {E} }(B_{r}\left(x\right))}},\absolutevalue{\nu_E \xp}=1\right\},

where we refer to
\nu_{E}\left(x\right)
as the measure-theoretical unit normal at
x\in\partial E
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, if
\nu_{E}\left(x\right)
exists
T_{x,\nu_{E}\left(x\right)}:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y\cdot\nu_{E}\left(x\right)=0\right\}
stands for the approximate tangent line and
H_{x,\nu_{E}\left(x\right)}:=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y\cdot\nu_{E}\left(x\right)\leq 0\right\}
is its corresponding half space at
x
.

For any set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of finite perimeter, by
[
43
, Corollary 15.8 and Theorem 16.2]
it yields that

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{(1/2)}\setminus\partial^{*}E)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial^{*}E\subset E^{(1/2)}.

Moreover, for any set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of finite perimeter, we have

(2.3)

\partial E=N\cup\partial^{*}E\cup(E^{(1)}\cup E^{(0)})\cap\partial E,

where
N
is a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible (see
[
43
, Section 16.1]
).

We denote by
U_{k,l}
the (half-open) dyadic square, i.e.,

U_{k,l}:=[0,2^{-k})\times_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[0,2^{-k})+2^{-k}l

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
. We denote by
\mathcal{Q}
the family of dyadic squares and by
\mathcal{N}^{1}

the net measure
. More precisely, for any Borel set
E
,

(2.4)

\mathcal{N}^{1}(E):=\liminf_{\delta\to 0}{\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\delta}}(E),

is the
\mathcal{N}^{1}
-measure of
E
, where

(2.5)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{\delta}(E):=\inf\{\sum_{i\in I}\mathrm{diam}(U_{i}):\left\{U_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}\subset\mathcal{Q}\text{ is a countable disjoint covering of }E\\
\text{ and }\mathrm{diam}(U_{i})\leq\delta\}.

Note that this measure does not coincide with the Hausdorff measure, however we have the following equivalence (see
[
44
, Chapter 5]
)

(2.6)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\leq\mathcal{N}^{1}(E)\leq 2^{\frac{5}{2}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E),

for any Borel set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Functions of bounded pointwise variation

Given a function
h:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
we denote the pointwise variation of
h
by

\textrm{Var}\,h:=\sup{\left\{\sum_{i}^{n}\absolutevalue{ h(x_i) - h(x_{i-1})}:P:=\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}\}\ \textrm{is a partition of $[a,b]$}\right\}}.

We say that
h:(a,b)\to\mathbb{R}
has finite pointwise variation if
\textrm{Var}\,h<\infty.
We recall that for any function
h
such that
\textrm{Var}\,h<\infty
,
h
has at most countable discontinuities and there exists
h(x^{\pm}):=\lim_{z\to x^{\pm}}h(z)
. In the following given a function
h:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
with finite pointwise variation, we define

h^{-}(x):=\min\{h(x^{+}),h(x^{-})\}=\liminf_{z\to x}h(z)

and

h^{+}(x):=\max\{h(x^{+}),h(x^{-})\}=\limsup_{z\to x}h(z).

In view of
[
41
, Corollary 2.23]
and with slightly abuse of notation, the limits

(2.7)

h^{+}(a):=\lim_{x\to a^{-}}h(x)\quad\text{and}\quad h^{-}(b):=\lim_{x\to b^{-}}h(x)

are finite.

3.
Mathematical setting and main results

In this section we present the model introduced in this manuscript with some preliminaries, and then we state the main results of the paper outlining the consequences for the related one-phase setting of
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
and the multiple-phase setting of film multilayers considered in
[
42
]
.

3.1.
The two-phase model

Let
\Omega:=(-l,l)\times_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(-L,L)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
for positive parameters
l,L\in\mathbb{R}
. We begin by introducing the family
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
of admissible configurations and, in particular, the admissible substrate regions.

Roughly speaking, an admissible substrate region
S\subset\Omega
is characterized as the subgraph of a upper semicontinuous height function
h
with finite pointwise variation to which we subtract a closed
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable set
K
such that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)<\infty
, which represents the substrate internal cracks. More precisely, we consider the
family of admissible (substrate) heights

\mathrm{AH}(\Omega)
defined by

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{AH}(\Omega):=\left\{h:[-l,l]\to[0,L]:h\text{ is upper semicontinous and }\mathrm{Var}\,h<\infty\right\}

and let
S_{h}
denote the closed subgraph with height
h\in\mathrm{AH}(\Omega)
, i.e.,

(3.2)

S_{h}:=\{(x,y):-l<x<l,y\leq h(x)\}.

We then define the
family of admissible (substrate) cracks

\text{AK}(\Omega)
by

(3.3)

\mathrm{AK}(\Omega):=\{K\subset\Omega:\text{ $K$ is a closed set in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$,
$\mathcal{H}^{1}$-rectifiable and }\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)<\infty\}

and the
family of pairs of admissible heights and cracks

\text{AHK}(\Omega)
by

(3.4)

\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega):=\{(h,K)\in\mathrm{AH}(\Omega)\times\mathrm{AK}(\Omega):\,K\subset{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(S_{h})}}\}.

Finally, given
(h,K)\in\text{AHK}(\Omega)
we refer to the set

(3.5)

S_{h,K}:={({S_{h}}\setminus K)}\cap\Omega,

as the
substrate with height
h
and cracks
K
,
and we define the
family of admissible substrates
as

(3.6)

\mathrm{AS}(\Omega):=\{S\subset\Omega\,:\,\text{$S=S_{h,K}$ for a pair $(h,K)\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)$}\}.

We observe that

(3.7)

\partial{S_{h,K}}=\partial{S_{h}}\cup K

for every
S_{h,K}\in\mathrm{AS}(\Omega)
,
so that
\overline{S_{h,K}}=S_{h}
and
{\rm{Int}}(S_{h,K})={\rm{Int}}(S_{h})\setminus K
. We denote the
jumps points
and the
vertical filament points
of the graph of
h\in{\rm AH}(\Omega)
by

(3.8)

J(h):=\{x\in(-l,l):h^{-}(x)\neq h^{+}(x)\}\quad\text{and}\quad F(h):=\{x\in(-l,l):h^{+}(x)<h(x)\},

respectively.
By
[
41
, Corollary 2.23]
and thanks to the fact that
h\in{\rm AH}(\Omega)
,
J(h)
and
F(h)
are countable.
Moreover, it follows that
\partial{S_{h}}
is connected and,
\partial S_{h}
and
\partial S_{h,K}
have finite
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measure. By
[
25
, Lemma 3.12 and Lemma 3.13]
, for any
h\in\mathrm{AH}
,
\partial S_{h}
is rectifiable and applying the Besicovitch-Marstrand-Mattila Theorem (see
[
4
, Theorem 2.63]
),
\partial{S_{h}}
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, and hence,
\partial S_{h,K}
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable. Furthermore, applying
[
37
, Proposition A.1]

S_{h}
and
S_{h,K}
are sets of finite perimeter.

The following result allows to interchangeably bound the pointwise variation of a function
h\in{\rm AH}(\Omega)
from the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measure of
\partial S_{h}
, and vice versa.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
h\in{\rm AH}(\Omega)
. Then

(3.9)

\mathrm{Var}\,h\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S_{h})\leq 2l+2\mathrm{Var}\,h,

where
S_{h}
is defined as in (
3.2
).

Proof.

The proof is divided in two steps.

Step 1.
We prove the left inequality of (
3.9
). We proceed as in
[
14
, Section A.2.3]
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\{l_{i}:i\in\{0,\ldots,m\},l_{0}=-l,l_{m}=l\text{ and }l_{i}<l_{i+1}\}
be a partition of
[-l,l]
. Take
i\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}
and define by
L_{i}
the segment connecting
(l_{i},h(l_{i}))
with
(l_{i+1},h(l_{i+1}))
. By
[
25
, Lemma 3.12]
, there exists a parametrization
r_{i}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
of
\partial{\rm{Int}}(S_{h})\cap((l_{i},l_{i+1})\times_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[0,L))
, whose support
\gamma_{i}
joins the points
(l_{i},h(l_{i}))
and
(l_{i+1},h(l_{i+1}))
, furthermore, it follows that

\absolutevalue{ h(l_{i+1}) - h(l_{i})}\leq\sqrt{\absolutevalue{l_{i+1} - l_i}^{2}+\absolutevalue{ h(l_{i+1}) - h(l_{i})}^{2}}=\mathcal{H}^{1}(L_{i})\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\gamma_{i}).

Moreover, repeating the same argument for any
i\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}
we have that

\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\absolutevalue{ h(l_{i+1}) - h(l_{i})}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\gamma_{i})\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S_{h}),

where in the last inequality we have used that

(3.10)

\partial S_{h}=\partial{\rm{Int}}(S_{h})\cup(\partial S_{h}\cap S_{h}^{(0)})\cup N,

where
N
is a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible set. Taking the supremum aver all partitions of we obtain the left inequality of (
3.9
).

Step 2.
In this step, we prove the right inequality of (
3.9
). We observe that

{\rm{Int}}(S_{h})=\{(x,y):-l<x<l,y<h^{-}(x)\}

and so,

\begin{matrix}r:&[-l,l]&\to&\partial{\rm{Int}}(S_{h})\\
&x&\mapsto&(x,h^{-}(x))\end{matrix}

is a parametrization of
\partial{\rm{Int}}(S_{h})
, whose support we denote by
\gamma
. Therefore, from
[
41
, Definition 4.6 and Remark 4.20]
it follows that

(3.11)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{\rm{Int}}(S_{h}))=\sup\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\absolutevalue{r(x_{i+1}) - r(x_i)}\right\},

where the supremum is taken over all partitions of
[-l,l]
. By definition of
r
and thanks to (
3.11
), we see that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{\rm{Int}}(S_{h}))\leq\sup\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\left(\absolutevalue{x_{i+1} - x_i}+\absolutevalue{h^-(x_{i+1}) - h^-(x_i)}\right)\right\}\leq 2l+\mathrm{Var}\,h,

where we used the fact that
\mathrm{Var}\,h^{-}\leq\mathrm{Var}\,h
. Finally, by (
3.10
) we have that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S_{h})=\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{\rm{Int}}(S_{h}))+\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S_{h}\cap S_{h}^{(0)})
and since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S_{h}\cap S_{h}^{(0)})\leq\mathrm{Var}\,h
, by the fact that
\partial S_{h}\cap S_{h}^{(0)}
is the union of vertical segments, we can deduce the right inequality of (
3.9
).
∎

We now introduce the family of admissible region pairs and configurations.

Definition 3.2

(Admissible regions and configurations)
.

We define the families of admissible pairs
\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
and of admissible configurations
\mathcal{C}
by

\mathcal{B}(\Omega):=\{(A,S):A\text{ is }\mathcal{L}^{2}\text{-measurable,}\,\partial A\textrm{ is $\mathcal{H}^{1}$-rectifiable, }\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial A)<\infty,\,\\
{\text{there exists }}(h,K)\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega),\,S=S_{h,K}\in\mathrm{AS}(\Omega),\,\\
S_{h,K}\subset\overline{A}\subset\overline{\Omega}\text{ and }\partial A\cap{\rm{Int}}(S_{h,K})=\emptyset\},

and

{{\mathcal{C}}}:=\left\{\left(A,S,u\right){\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}:{(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}}\right\},

respectively.

In the following we also refer to the sets
\overline{A}
,
\overline{S}
, and
\overline{A}\setminus S^{(1)}
with respect to an admissible pair
(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}
as the
composite region
, the
substrate region
, and the
film region
of the admissible pair. Moreover, we refer to
S^{(1)}
and
A^{(1)}\cap S^{0}
as the
substrate
and the
film bulk regions
, respectively.

In Theorem
3.12
we will need to consider a natural extension of the families
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, which we denote as
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively.

Definition 3.3
.

We define the families of admissible pairs
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Omega)
and of admissible configurations
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
by

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Omega):=\{(A,S):A,S\text{ are }\mathcal{L}^{2}\text{-measurable,}\,\partial A,\partial S\textrm{ are $\mathcal{H}^{1}$-rectifiable, }\\
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial A),\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S)<\infty,\,S\subset\overline{A}\subset\overline{\Omega}\text{ and }\partial A\cap{\rm{Int}}(S)=\emptyset\},

and

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}:=\left\{\left(A,S,u\right):(A,S)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}},u\in{H^{1}_{\mathrm{{loc}}}\left({\rm{Int}}(A);\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)}\right\},

respectively.

We observe that
\mathcal{B}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
, since for any
(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}
there exists
(h,K)\in\text{AHK}(\Omega)
such that
S=S_{h,K}\in\text{AS}(\Omega)
and
\partial S_{h,K}
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, and thus,
(A,S)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Omega)
.

Notice that, for simplicity, in the absence of ambiguity we omit the dependence on the set
\Omega
in the notation
{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Omega)\,}
and
\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
by writing in the following only
{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\,}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively.

Remark 3.4
.

We observe that any bounded
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial A)<\infty
is a set of finite perimeter in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by
[
37
, Proposition A.1]
.

We now equip the family
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
with a topology.

Definition 3.5

(
{\tau_{\mathcal{B}}}
-convergence)
.

A sequence
\{(A_{k},S_{k})\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}

{\tau_{\mathcal{B}}}
-converges to
(A,S)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
,
if

-

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(\partial A_{k}\right)}<\infty
,
\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(\partial S_{k}\right)}<\infty
,

-

\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial A_{k}\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial A\right)
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
,

-

\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial S_{k}\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{sdist}\left(\cdot,\partial S\right)
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
.

It will follow from Lemma
3.9

below that the
\tau_{B}
-convergence is closed in the subfamily of admissible triples
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}\subset\mathcal{B}
whose definition depending on the vector
{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
we now provide.

Definition 3.6
.

For any
\mathbf{m}:={(m_{0},m_{1})}\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
the family
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
is given by all pairs
(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}

such that
\partial A
and
\partial S

have at most
m_{1}
and
m_{0}
-connected components, respectively. Let us also define

(3.12)

\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}:=\left\{\left(A,{S,}u\right)\in\mathcal{C}:(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\subset\mathcal{C}.

We denote the topology with which we equip the family
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
by
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Definition 3.7

(
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
-Convergence)
.

A sequence
\{\left(A_{k},{S_{k}},u_{k}\right)\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{C}
is said to
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
-convergence to
\left(A,S,u\right)\in\mathcal{C}
, denoted as
\left(A_{k},{S_{k}},u_{k}\right)\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(A,S,u\right)
, if

-

(A_{k},{S_{k}})\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{B}}}(A,S)
,

-

u_{k}\rightarrow u
a.e. in
\mathrm{{Int}\left(A\right)}
.

We now state some properties of the topology
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Remark 3.8
.

We notice that:

(i)

The following assertions are equivalent

(i.
1
)

{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E_{k})\rightarrow{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E)
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(i.
2
)

E_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\overline{E}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E)
.

Moreover, these imply that
\partial E_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\partial E
.

(ii)

If there exist
(h_{k},K_{k})\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
and
(h,K)\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
such that
E_{k}=E_{h_{k},K_{k}}\in{\rm AS}(\Omega)
and
E=E_{h,K}\in AS
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we observe that Item (i.
1
) above is equivalent to

E_{k}=E_{h_{k},K_{k}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}E_{h}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{k}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus E_{h_{k},K_{k}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E_{h}))\cup K,

where
E_{h_{k},K_{k}}
,
E_{h,K}
are defined as in (
3.5
) and
E_{h}
is defined as in (
3.2
).

(iii)

Let
\{(E_{k},F_{k})\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a sequence of bounded sets and let
E,F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be two bounded sets such that
\partial E_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\partial E
and
\partial F_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\partial F
. In view of the Kuratowski convergence (see
[
4
, Section 6.1]
,
[
18
, Chapter 4]
or
[
36
, Appendix A.1]
), we observe that for every
x\in\partial E\setminus\partial F
there exist
r:=r(x)>0
and
k_{r,x}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
B(x,r)\cap\partial F_{k}=\emptyset
for any
k\geq k_{r,x}
. Similarly, for every
x\in\partial F\setminus\partial E
there exists
r^{\prime}:=r^{\prime}(x)>0
and
k_{r^{\prime},x}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
B(x,r)\cap\partial E_{k}=\emptyset
for any
k\geq k_{r^{\prime},x}
.

From the next result the closedness and the compactness (see Theorem
4.2
)
of the family
\mathcal{B}_{\textbf{m}}
with respect to the topology
\tau_{\mathcal{B}}
follows for every
\textbf{m}:=(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\{E_{k}\}
be a sequence of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-measurable subsets of
\overline{\Omega}
having
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable boundaries
\partial E_{k}
with at most
m
-connected components such that

-

\sup_{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial E_{k})<\infty
,

-

{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E_{k})\to{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E)
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
k\to\infty
for a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Then,
\partial E
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-finite,
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, and with at most
m
-connected components, and
E\subset\overline{\Omega}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-measurable. Furthermore, if
E_{k}=E_{h_{k},K_{k}}\in\mathrm{AS}(\Omega)
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for some
(h_{k},K_{k})\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
, then

(3.13)

\sup_{k}(\mathcal{H}^{1}(K_{k})+\mathrm{Var}\,h_{k})<\infty

and there exists
(h,K)\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
such that
E=E_{h,K}\in\mathrm{AS}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

The fact that
\partial E
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-finite,
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, and with at most
m
-connected components, is a direct consequence of
[
36
, Lemma 3.2]
. Since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial E)<\infty
, it follows that
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\overline{E}\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E))=\mathcal{L}^{2}(\partial E)=0
, by applying
[
6
, Theorem 14.5]
to
E\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E)\subset\overline{E}\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E)
we infer that
E\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E)
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-measurable and so,
\mathcal{L}^{2}(E\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E))=0
. Therefore,
E=E\setminus{\rm{Int}}(E)\cup{\rm{Int}}(E)
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-measurable.

It remains to prove the last assertion of the statement. Let
(h_{k},K_{k})\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
such that
E_{k}=E_{h_{k},K_{k}}\in\mathrm{AS}(\Omega)
for every
k
. We begin by observing that (
3.13
) is a direct consequence of (
3.7
), by applying (
3.9
) to
h_{k}
. To conclude the proof we proceed in 2 steps.

Step 1.
We claim that

\overline{E}=E_{h}
, where
h
is the upper semicontinous function defined by

h(x_{1}):=\sup\{\limsup_{k\to\infty}h_{k}(x_{1}^{k}):x^{k}_{1}\to x_{1}\}.

Let
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\overline{E}
, by Remark
3.8
-(i) we observe that there exists
x_{k}=(x_{1}^{k},x_{2}^{k})\in E_{k}
such that
x_{k}\to x
. We deduce that

x_{2}=\lim_{k\to\infty}x^{k}_{2}\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}h_{k}(x_{1}^{k})\leq h(x_{1}),

and by (
3.2
) we deduce that
\overline{E}\subset E_{h}
. Now let
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in E_{h}
, by definition we observe that

x_{2}\leq h(x_{1}):=\sup\{\limsup_{k\to\infty}h_{k}(x^{k}_{1}):x^{k}_{1}\to x_{1}\}.

Let
x_{k}=(x^{k}_{1},x^{k}_{2})\in\Omega
such that
x^{k}_{1}\to x_{1}
,
h_{k}(x^{k}_{1})\to h(x_{1})
and define
x_{2}^{k}:=\min\{x_{2},h_{k}(x^{k}_{1})\}
. It follows that
x_{k}\in\overline{E_{k}}
and
x_{k}\to x
, by Kuratowski convergence we have that
x\in\overline{E}
, therefore,
E_{h}\subset\overline{E}
.

Step 2.
We claim that
{\rm{Int}}(E)={\rm{Int}}({E_{h,K}})
, where
K:=\partial E\cap{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})}}
.
Notice that
K
is a closed set in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and since
\partial E
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, we deduce that
K
is also
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable.
On one hand, we see that

{\rm{Int}}(E)={\rm{Int}}(E)\setminus(\partial E\cap{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})}})\subset{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})\setminus(\partial E\cap{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})}})=:{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})\setminus K={\rm{Int}}({E_{h,K}}),

where in the first equality we used the fact that
{\rm{Int}}(E)\cap\partial E=\emptyset
and in the inclusion we used Step 1 and the fact that
E\subset\overline{E}=E_{h}
. On the other hand, let
x\in{\rm{Int}}(E_{h,K})={\rm{Int}}(E_{h})\setminus(\partial E\cap{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})}})
and assume by contradiction that
x\notin{\rm{Int}}(E)
. This assumption implies that either
x\in\partial E
or
x\in\Omega\setminus\overline{E}
, which is a contradiction by the facts that
\partial E\subset\overline{E}=E_{h}
and

x\in{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})\setminus(\partial E\cap{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})}})={\rm{Int}}(E_{h})\setminus\partial E\subset E_{h}=\overline{E}.

Finally, observe that
(h,K)\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
. Thanks to the uniqueness of Kuratowski convergence, the facts that
\overline{E_{h,K}}=E_{h}
and
{\rm{Int}}(E_{h,K})={\rm{Int}}(E_{h})\setminus(\partial E\cap{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(E_{h})}})
, and in view of Remark
3.8
-(i) we conclude from the previous two steps that
E=E_{h,K}
.
∎

The total energy
\mathcal{F}:\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\left[0,+\infty\right]
of admissible configurations is given as the sum of two contributions, namely the surface energy
\mathcal{S}
and the elastic energy
\mathcal{W}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{F}(A,S,u):=\mathcal{S}(A,S)+\mathcal{W}(A,u)

for any
(A,S,u)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, where we observe that the surface energy does not depend on the displacements (as a difference from
[
36
,
37
]
). The surface energy
\mathcal{S}
is defined for any
(A,S)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
by

(3.14)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{S}}(A,{S})&:=\int_{\Omega\cap(\partial^{*}A\setminus\partial^{*}{S})}{\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(x,\nu_{A}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\Omega\cap\partial^{*}{S}\cap\partial^{*}A}{\varphi(x,\nu_{A}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega\cap(\partial^{*}{S}\setminus\partial A)\cap A^{(1)}}{\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}(x,\nu_{S}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\Omega\cap\partial^{*}{S}\cap\partial A\cap A^{(1)}}{(\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}+\varphi)(x,\nu_{A}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega\cap\partial A\cap A^{(1)}\cap S^{(0)}}{2\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(x,\nu_{A}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\Omega\cap\partial A\cap A^{(0)}}{2\varphi^{\prime}(x,\nu_{A}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega\cap(\partial{S}\setminus\partial A)\cap\left(S^{(1)}\cup S^{(0)}\right)\cap A^{(1)}}{2\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}(x,\nu_{S}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega\cap\partial{S}\cap\partial A\cap S^{(1)}}{2\varphi(x,\nu_{A}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}},\end{split}

where
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}},\,\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}:\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,\infty]
and, given also the function
\varphi_{\mathrm{S}}:\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,\infty]
, we define the functions
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
in in

C(\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{2};[0,\infty])
by

\varphi:=\min\{\varphi_{\mathrm{S}},\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi^{\prime}:=\min\{\varphi_{\mathrm{F}},\varphi_{\mathrm{S}}\}.

Notice that
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}},\varphi_{\mathrm{S}},\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}
represent the anisotropic surface tensions of the film/vapor, the substrate/vapor and the substrate/film interfaces, respectively, while
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are referred to as the anisotropic
regime surface tensions
and are introduced to include into the analysis the wetting and dewetting regimes. We refer the Reader to the Introduction for related explanation and for the motivation for the integral densities choice in (
3.14
).

Similarly to
[
21
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
, by also taking into account that in our setting the film and substrate regions are given as subsets of the composite regions, the elastic energy is defined for configurations
\left(A,S,u\right)\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}
by

\mathcal{W}(A,u):=\int_{A}{W\left(x,E\left(u\left(x\right)-E_{0}\left(x\right)\right)\right)dx},

where the elastic density
W
is determined by the quadratic form

W\left(x,M\right):=\mathbb{C}\left(x\right)M:M,

for a fourth-order tensor
\mathbb{C}:\Omega\to\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\rm sym}
,
E
denotes the symmetric gradient, i.e.,
E(v):=\frac{\gradient v+\gradient^{T}v}{2}
for any
v\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{{loc}}}(\Omega)
and
E_{0}
is
the mismatch strain
x\in\Omega\mapsto E_{0}\left(x\right)\in\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\mathrm{{sym}}}
defined as

E_{0}:=\begin{cases}E(u_{0})&\text{in }\Omega\setminus S,\\
0&\text{in }S,\end{cases}

for a fixed
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
.

3.2.
Main results

We state here the main results of the paper and the connection to the one-phase and multiple-phase settings.

Fix
l,L>0
and consider
\Omega:=(-l,l)\times_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(-L,L)
. Let
\varphi:=\min\{\varphi_{\mathrm{S}},\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}\},\,\varphi^{\prime}:=\min\{\varphi_{\mathrm{F}},\varphi_{\mathrm{S}}\}
for three functions
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}},\varphi_{\mathrm{S}},\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}:\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,\infty]
.
We assume throughout the paper that:

(H1)

\varphi_{\mathrm{F}},\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}},\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in C(\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{2};[0,\infty])
are Finsler norms such that

(3.15)

c_{1}\absolutevalue{\xi}\leq\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(x,\xi),{\varphi}(x,\xi),\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}(x,\xi)\leq c_{2}\absolutevalue{\xi}.

for every
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and for two constants
0<c_{1}\leq c_{2}
.

(H2)

We have

(3.16)

\varphi(x,\xi)\geq|\varphi_{\textrm{FS}}(x,\xi)-\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(x,\xi)|

for every
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(H3)

\mathbb{C}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\rm sym})
and there exists
c_{3}>0
such that

(3.17)

\mathbb{C}\left(x\right)M:M\geq 2c_{3}M:M

for every
M\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{{sym}}}^{2\times 2}.

We notice that under assumptions (H1)-(H3), the energy
\mathcal{F}(A,S,u)\in[0,\infty]
for every
(A,S,u)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
.

The main result of the paper is the following existence result.

Theorem 3.10

(Existence of minimizers)
.

Assume (H1)-(H3) and let
\mathbbm{v}_{0},\mathbbm{v}_{1}\in[\mathcal{L}^{2}({\Omega}/2),\mathcal{L}^{2}({\Omega}))
such that
\mathbbm{v}_{0}\leq\mathbbm{v}_{1}
. Then for every
\mathbf{m}=(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
the volume constrained minimum problem

(3.18)

\inf_{(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}},\,\mathcal{L}^{2}({A})=\mathbbm{v}_{1},\,\mathcal{L}^{2}({S_{h,K}})=\mathbbm{v}_{0}}{\mathcal{F}(A,S,u)}

and the unconstrained minimum problem

(3.19)

\inf_{(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}}{\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}(A,S,u)}

have solution, where
\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}:\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\mathcal{F}^{\lambda}(A,S,u):=\mathcal{F}(A,S,u)+\lambda_{1}\absolutevalue{ \mathcal{L}^2({A})- \mathbbm{v}_1 }+\lambda_{0}\absolutevalue{ \mathcal{L}^2({S})- \mathbbm{v}_0 },

for any
{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}),
with
\lambda_{0}
,
\lambda_{1}>0
.

To prove Theorem
3.10
we apply the direct method of calculus of variations. On the one hand, in Section
4
, we show that any energy equi-bounded sequence
\{(A_{k},S_{k},u_{k})\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
satisfy the following compactness property.

Theorem 3.11

(Compactness in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
)
.

Assume (H1) and (H3). Let
\{(A_{k},{S_{h_{k},K_{k}}},u_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
be such that

(3.20)

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{F}(A_{k},{S_{h_{k},K_{k}}},u_{k})}<\infty.

Then, there exist an admissible configuration
(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
of finite energy, a subsequence
\{\left(A_{k_{n}},S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}},u_{k_{n}}\right)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, a sequence
\left\{\left(\widetilde{A}_{n},{\widetilde{S}_{n}},u_{k_{n}}\right)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
and a sequence
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of piecewise rigid displacements associated to
\widetilde{A}_{n}
such that
\left(\widetilde{A}_{n},{\widetilde{S}_{n}},u_{k_{n}}+b_{n}\right)\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{C}}}(A,S,u)
,
\mathcal{L}^{2}(A_{k_{n}})=\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widetilde{A}_{n})
,
\mathcal{L}^{2}(S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}})=\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widetilde{S}_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

(3.21)

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{F}(A_{k_{n}},S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}},u_{k_{n}})}=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{F}\left(\widetilde{A}_{n},{\widetilde{S}_{n}},u_{k_{n}}+b_{n}\right)}.

On the other hand, in Section
5
we show that
\mathcal{F}
is lower semicontinuous in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
with respect to the topology
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Theorem 3.12

(Lower semicontinuity of
\mathcal{F}
)
.

Assume (H1)-(H3). Let
\{\left(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}},u_{k}\right)\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
and
\left(A,S_{h,K},u\right)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
be such that
\left(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}},u_{k}\right)\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(A,S_{h,K},u\right)
. Then

(3.22)

\mathcal{F}\left(A,S_{h,K},u\right)\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{F}\left(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}},u_{k}\right)}.

We now describe the consequences for the one-phase setting of the results obtained in this manuscript for the two-phase setting.

Remark 3.13

(Relation to literature models with fixed substrate)
.

The energy considered in this paper can be seen as an extension of the energies previously considered in the literature, e.g., in
[
36
,
27
,
26
]
, by “fixing the substrate regions”.
More precisely, if we consider the subfamily
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}
where

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:=\{(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}:\,\overline{\partial S\cap\Omega}\text{ is a Lipschitz 1-manifold}\}

and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\{(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}:\,(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\}
, then the energy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
defined for every
(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\left(A,S,u\right)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}\left(A,S,u\right)-\int_{\Omega\cap\partial^{*}{S}}{\varphi_{\mathrm{FS}}\left(z,\nu_{S}(z)\right)\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}-\int_{\Omega\cap(\partial A\setminus\partial{S})\cap A^{(0)}}{2{\varphi^{\prime}}(z,\nu_{A}(z))d\mathcal{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\cap\partial^{*}A\setminus{\partial{S}}}{\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(z,\nu_{A}(z))d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\Omega\cap(\partial A\setminus\partial{S})\cap\cup A^{(1)}}{2\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}(z,\nu_{A}(z))d\mathcal{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega\cap\partial^{*}{S}\cap\partial A\cap A^{(1)}}{(\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}-\beta)(x,\nu_{A}\left(x\right))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}}-\int_{\Omega\cap\partial^{*}{S}\cap\partial^{*}A}{\beta(z,\nu_{A}(z))d\mathcal{H}^{1}},

where
\beta:=\varphi_{\textrm{FS}}-\varphi
, is analogous to
the energy
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
of
[
36
, Theorem 2.9]
(where the notation
\varphi
was referring to
\varphi_{\mathrm{F}}
), which is an extension of the energies of
[
27
,
26
]
as described in
[
36
, Remark 2.10]
. We notice though that, even in the situation of a fixed regular substrate, i.e., by considering the family
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}:=\{(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}:\,S=S_{0}\}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

for a fixed admissible region
S_{0}
such that
\overline{\partial S_{0}\cap\Omega}
is a Lipschitz 1-manifold,
the setting considered in this manuscript allows to include into the analysis the possibility of an uncountable number of film islands (or film voids) on top of the substrate (which was instead precluded in
[
36
]
), because of the crucial difference introduced in the setting of this manuscript consisting of always including the substrate regions inside the admissible region
A
(with the film region then being represented by
\overline{A}\setminus S^{(1)}
). We also notice that the hypotheses on the surface tensions in this manuscript coincide with the ones in
[
36
]
up to the observation that on the right hand-side of (
3.16
) one can disregard the absolute value for the setting with a fixed substrate).

We conclude the section by outlining the consequences of the results obtained in this manuscript for the two-phase setting in the multiple-phase setting of film multilayers that is the object of investigation in
[
42
]
.

Remark 3.14

(Relation to the setting of film multilayers)
.

In the parallel paper
[
42
]
the authors consider the setting in which also the film region is subject to a graph constraint, by introducing a family of admissible regions for the film and the substrate of the form
\mathcal{B}^{1}:=\{(S_{h^{1},K^{1}},S_{h^{0},K^{0}})\in\mathrm{AS}(\Omega)\times\mathrm{AS}(\Omega):\,h^{0}\leq h^{1}\text{ and }\partial S_{h^{1},K^{1}}\cap{\rm{Int}}(S_{h^{0},K^{0}})=\emptyset\}\subset\mathcal{B}
and the related family
\mathcal{C}^{1}:=\{(S_{h^{1},K^{1}},S_{h^{0},K^{0}},u):\,(S_{h^{1},K^{1}},S_{h^{0},K^{0}})\in\mathcal{B}^{1}\text{ and }u\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S_{h^{1},K^{1}};\mathbb{R}^{2})\}\subset\mathcal{C}
of admissible configurations. By implementing in the compactness for both the substrate and the film the arguments employed in this manuscript for the substrate, a similar result to Theorem
3.10
is established, thus providing an existence result for the problem of films resting on deformable substrates in the presence of delaminations, which can be seen as an extension of
[
21
,
22
,
27
]
. Furthermore, in
[
42
]
by then performing also an iteration procedure an existence result is provided also for the setting of finitely-many film multilayers.

4.
Compactness

In this section, we fix
\mathbf{m}:=(m_{0},m_{1})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
and we prove that the families
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
are compact with respect to
\tau_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
topologies, respectively.

Proposition 4.1
.

The following assertions hold:

(i)

For every sequence of closed sets
\{E_{k}\}
, there exists
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
E_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}E
.

(ii)

For every sequence
\{E_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
there exists a subsequence
\{E_{k_{l}}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
and
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathrm{{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E_{k_{l}})\rightarrow\mathrm{{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial E)
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The proof of Proposition
4.1
-(i) and -(ii) can be found in
[
4
, Theorem 6.1]
and
[
36
, Theorem 6.1]
, respectively (see also
[
4
, Theorem 6.1]
for the version of Item (ii) with the signed-distance convergence replaced by the
Hausdorff-metric convergence
).

Theorem 4.2

(Compactness of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
)
.

Let
\{(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}})\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
such that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{S}(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}})}<\infty

for
(h_{k},K_{k})\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
. Then, there exist a not relabeled subsequence
\{(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}})\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
and
(A,S_{h,K})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
such that
(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}})\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{B}}}(A,S_{h,K})
.

Proof.

For simplicity we denote
S_{k}:=S_{h_{k},K_{k}}
.
We begin by observing that by Proposition
4.1
-(ii) there exist
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathrm{{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial A_{k_{l}})\rightarrow\mathrm{{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial A)
and
\mathrm{{sdist}}\left(\cdot,\partial{S_{{k_{l}}}}\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial{S})
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
R:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{S}(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}})}
.
In view of Remark
3.4
and by (
2.3
) we have the following decomposition of
\partial A_{k}
,

\displaystyle\partial A_{k}

\displaystyle=\partial^{*}A_{k}\cup(\partial A_{k}\cap(A_{k}^{(0)}\cup A_{k}^{(1)}))\cup N_{k},

where
N_{k}
is a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible set for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
we observe that

(4.1)

\displaystyle\partial A_{k}\setminus\partial{S_{k}}=\partial^{*}A_{k}\setminus\partial{S_{k}}\cup\left((\partial A_{k}\setminus\partial{S_{k}})\cap\left(A_{k}^{(0)}\cup A_{k}^{(1)}\right)\right)\cup N^{\prime}_{k},

where
N^{\prime}_{k}:=N_{k}\setminus\partial{S_{k}}
is a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible set. Since for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
S_{k}
is a set of finite perimeter, by (
2.3
) we have that

\displaystyle\partial S_{k}

\displaystyle=\partial^{*}S_{k}\cup(\partial S_{k}\cap(S_{k}^{(0)}\cup S_{k}^{(1)}))\cup\widetilde{N}_{k},

where
\widetilde{N}_{k}
is a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible set for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Reasoning similarly to (
4.1
) we have that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\partial S_{k}\setminus\partial{A_{k}}

\displaystyle=\partial^{*}S_{k}\setminus\partial{A_{k}}\cup(\partial S_{k}\setminus\partial{A_{k}})\cap\left(S_{k}^{(0)}\cup S_{k}^{(1)}\right)\cup\widetilde{N}^{\prime}_{k},

(4.3)

\displaystyle(\partial{S_{k}}\setminus\partial A_{k})\cap A_{k}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\partial^{*}{S_{k}}\setminus\partial A_{k}\cup\left(\partial{S_{k}}\setminus\partial A_{k}\cap\left(S^{(1)}_{k}\cup S_{k}^{(0)}\right)\right)\right)\cap A_{k}^{(1)}\cup\widetilde{N}^{\prime\prime}_{k}

and

(4.4)

\displaystyle\partial{S_{k}}\cap\partial{A_{k}}\cap A_{k}^{(1)}=\left((\partial^{*}{S_{k}}\cap\partial{A_{k}})\cup\left((\partial{S_{k}}\cap\partial{A_{k}})\cap(S^{(1)}_{k}\cup S_{k}^{(0)})\right)\right)\cap A_{k}^{(1)}\cup\widetilde{N}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k},

where
\widetilde{N}^{\prime}_{k}
,
\widetilde{N}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
and
\widetilde{N}^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}
are
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible sets for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore, we can deduce that

(4.5)

\begin{split}\partial S_{k}\cap\partial A_{k}&=\left(\partial^{*}S_{k}\cup(\partial S_{k}\cap(S_{k}^{(0)}\cup S_{k}^{(1)}))\right)\cap\left(\partial^{*}A_{k}\cup(\partial A_{k}\cap(A_{k}^{(0)}\cup A_{k}^{(1)}))\right)\cup\widehat{N}_{k}\\
&=\left(\partial S_{k}^{*}\cap\partial^{*}A_{k}\right)\cup\left(\partial S_{k}\cap\partial^{*}A_{k}\cap(S_{k}^{(0)}\cup S_{k}^{(1)})\right)\cup\left(\partial^{*}S_{k}\cap\partial A_{k}\cap(A_{k}^{(0)}\cup A_{k}^{(1)})\right)\\
&\quad\left(\partial S_{k}\cap\partial A_{k}\cap((A_{k}^{(0)}\cup A_{k}^{(1)})\cup(S_{k}^{(0)}\cup S_{k}^{(1)}))\right)\cup\widehat{N}_{k},\end{split}

where
\widehat{N}_{k}
is a
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-negligible set, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. By (H1),(H3), (
4.1
)-(
4.5
) and thanks to the fact that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
S_{k}\subset\overline{A_{k}}
we have that

(4.6)

\begin{split}&c_{1}\left(\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial A_{k})+\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{S_{k}}\setminus\partial A_{k})\right)\\
&\quad\leq c_{1}\left(\int_{\partial^{*}{A_{k}}\setminus\partial{S_{k}}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\partial^{*}{S_{k}}\cap\partial^{*}{A_{k}}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\right.+\int_{(\partial{A_{k}}\setminus\partial{S_{k}})\cap({A_{k}}^{(0)}\cup{A_{k}}^{(1)})}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\\
&\qquad+\int_{(\partial^{*}{S_{k}}\setminus\partial{A_{k}})\cap{A_{k}}^{(1)}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\partial{S_{k}}\cap\partial{A_{k}}\cap{S_{k}}^{(1)}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\partial{S_{k}}\cap\partial^{*}{A_{k}}\cap{S_{k}}^{(0)}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\\
&\qquad+\int_{(\partial{S_{k}}\setminus\partial A_{k})\cap({S_{k}}^{(1)}\cup{S_{k}}^{(0)})\cap{A_{k}}^{(1)}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\int_{\partial^{*}{S_{k}}\cap\partial A_{k}\cap{A_{k}}^{(1)}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\\
&\qquad+\left.\int_{(\partial{S_{k}}\cap\partial A_{k})\cap{S_{k}}^{(0)}\cap{A_{k}}^{(1)}}{d\mathcal{H}^{1}}\right)\\
&\quad\leq 2({\mathcal{S}}(A_{k},{S_{k}}))\leq 2R,\end{split}

for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where in the first inequality we used Lemma
3.1
and in the second inequality we used (
3.15
). It follows that

(4.7)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial A_{k})\leq\frac{2R}{c_{1}}

and

(4.8)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{S_{k}})=\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{S_{k}}\cap\partial A_{k})+\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial{S_{k}}\setminus\partial A_{k})\leq\frac{2R}{c_{1}},

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

In view of (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) we conclude by Lemma
3.9
that
A\subset\overline{\Omega}
is
\mathcal{L}^{2}
-measurable,
\partial A
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-finite,
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, and with at most
m_{1}
-connected components, and that there exists
(h,K)\in\mathrm{AHK}(\Omega)
such that
S=S_{h,K}\in{\rm AS}(\Omega)
and
\partial S_{h,K}
has at most
m_{0}
-connected components. Furthermore, in view of Remark
3.8
-(i), since
\overline{S_{k}}\subset\overline{A_{k}}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

S_{h,K}\subset\overline{A}
.

In order to prove that
(A,S_{h,K})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
it remains to check that
\partial A\cap{\rm{Int}}(S_{h,k})=\emptyset
, to which the rest of the proof is devoted. Assume by contradiction that

(4.9)

\partial A\cap{\rm{Int}}(S_{h,k})\neq\emptyset.

Then, there exists
x\in\partial A\cap{\rm{Int}}(S_{h,k})
. By Remark
3.8
-(i), there exists
x_{k}\in\partial A_{k}
such that
x_{k}\to x
and hence, by
\tau_{\mathcal{B}}
-convergence we observe that

(4.10)

{\rm{sdist}}(x,\partial S_{h_{k},K_{k}})\to{\rm{sdist}}(x,\partial S_{h,K})\quad\text{as $k\to\infty.$}

Since by (
4.9
) there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\rm{sdist}}(x,\partial S_{h,K})=-\varepsilon,

we can find
k_{0}:=k_{0}(x)
for which
{\rm{sdist}}(x,\partial S_{h_{k_{0}},K_{k_{0}}})
is negative. Then,

x\in{\rm{Int}}(S_{h_{k_{0}},K_{k_{0}}})

and so, there exists
\delta\leq\varepsilon/2
such that

x_{k_{0}}\in B_{\delta}(x)\subset{\rm{Int}}(S_{h_{k_{0}},K_{k_{0}}}),

which is an absurd since
\partial A_{k}\cap{\rm{Int}}(S_{h_{k},K_{k}})=\emptyset
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

We are now in the position to prove Theorem
3.11
. To this end, we implement the arguments used in
[
36
, Theorem 2.7]
to the situation with free-boundary substrates and in particular the ones contained in Step 1 of the proof of
[
36
, Theorem 2.7]
. In fact, the original setting introduced in this paper with respect to
[
36
]
to model the delaminated interface regions allows to avoid the further modification of the film admissible regions that was performed in Steps 2 and 3 of
[
36
, Theorem 2.7]
.

Proof of Theorem
3.11
.

Denote
R:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{F}(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}},u_{k})}
.
Without loss of generality (by passing, if necessary, to a not relabeled subsequence), we assume that

(4.11)

\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{F}(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}},u_{k})}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathcal{F}(A_{k},S_{h_{k},K_{k}},u_{k})}\leq R.

Since
\mathcal{W}
is non-negative, by Theorem
4.2
there exist a subsequence
\{(A_{k_{n}},S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}})\}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
and
(A,S)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
such that
(A_{k_{n}},S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}})\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{B}}}(A,S)
. As a consequence of Theorem
4.2
, there exists
(h,K)\in\text{AHK}
such that
S=S_{h,K}
.

The rest of the proof is devoted to the construction of a sequence
(\widetilde{A}_{n},{\widetilde{S}_{n}})\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{m}}
to which we can apply
[
36
, Corollary 3.8]
(with
P={\rm{Int}}{(A)}
and
P_{n}={\rm{Int}}{(\widetilde{A}_{n})}
, respectively) in order to obtain
u\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\rm{Int}}{(A)};\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
(A,S,u)\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{m}}
has finite energy, and a sequence
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of piecewise rigid displacements such that
\left(\widetilde{A}_{n},{\widetilde{S}_{n}},u_{k_{n}}+b_{n}\right)\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{C}}}(A,S,u)
. Furthermore, we observe that also Equation (
3.21
) will be a consequence of such construction and hence, the assertion will directly follow.

By
[
36
, Proposition 3.6]
applied to
A_{k_{n}}
and
A
there exist a not relabeled subsequence
\{A_{k_{n}}\}
and a sequence
\{\widetilde{A}_{n}\}
with
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable boundary
\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}
of at most
m_{1}
-connected components such that

(4.12)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial\widetilde{A}_{n})<\infty,

that satisfy the following properties:

(a1)

\partial A_{k_{n}}\subset\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}
and
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}\setminus\partial A_{k_{n}})=0
,

(a2)

{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial\widetilde{A}_{n})\to{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial A)
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
n\to\infty
,

(a3)

if
\{E_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is the family of all connected components of
\mathrm{{Int}}(A),
there exist connected components of
\mathrm{{Int}}(\widetilde{A}_{n})
, which we enumerate as
\{E_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in I}
, such that for every
i
and
G\subset\subset E_{i}
one has that
G\subset\subset E_{i}^{n}
for all
n
large (depending only on
i
and
G
),

(a4)

\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widetilde{A}_{n})=\mathcal{L}^{2}(A_{k_{n}})
.

Furthermore, from the construction of
\widetilde{A}_{n}
(namely from the fact that
\widetilde{A}_{n}
is constructed by adding extra “internal” topological boundary to the selected subsequence
A_{k_{n}}
, see
[
36
, Propositions 3.4 and 3.6]
) it follows that

(4.13)

\widetilde{A}_{n}=A_{k_{n}}\setminus(\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}\setminus\partial A_{k_{n}})

with
\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}\setminus\partial A_{k_{n}}
given by a finite union of closed
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable sets connected to
\partial A_{k_{n}}
. More precisely, there exist a finite index set
J
and a family
\{\Gamma_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of closed
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable sets of
\Omega
connected to
\partial A_{k_{n}}
such that

\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}\setminus\partial A_{k_{n}}=\bigcup_{j\in J}\Gamma_{j}.

We define

\widetilde{K}_{n}:=K_{k_{n}}\cup((\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}\setminus\partial A_{k_{n}})\cap{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(S_{h_{k_{n}}})}})\subset{\overline{{\rm{Int}}(S_{h_{k_{n}}})}}

and we observe that
\widetilde{K}_{n}
is closed and
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable in view of the fact that
\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}\setminus\partial A_{k_{n}}
is a closed set in
\Omega
and is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, since
\partial\widetilde{A}_{n}
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-rectifiable. Therefore,
(h_{k_{n}},\widetilde{K}_{n})\in\text{AHK}(\Omega)
and
\widetilde{S}_{n}:=S_{h_{k_{n}},\widetilde{K}_{n}}\subset\overline{\widetilde{A}_{n}}
. We claim that
\partial{\widetilde{S}_{n}}
has at most
m_{0}
-connected
components, so that
(\widetilde{A}_{n},{\widetilde{S}_{n}})\in\mathcal{B}_{\textbf{m}}
. Indeed, if for every
j\in J
,
S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}\cap\Gamma_{j}
is empty there is nothing to prove, so we assume that there exists
j\in J
such that
S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}\cap\Gamma_{j}\neq\emptyset
. On one hand if
\Gamma_{j}\subset S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}
, thanks to the facts that
\Gamma_{j}
is connected to
\partial A_{k_{n}}
and
S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}\subset\overline{A_{k_{n}}}
, we deduce that
\Gamma_{j}
needs to be connected to
\partial S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}
. On the other hand, if
\Gamma_{j}\cap(A_{k_{n}}\setminus S_{h_{k_{n}}})\neq\emptyset
, then
we can find
x_{1}\in\Gamma_{j}\cap S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}
and
x_{2}\in\Gamma_{j}\cap(A_{k_{n}}\setminus S_{h_{k_{n}}})
. Since
\Gamma_{j}
is closed and connected, by
[
25
, Lemma 3.12]
there exists a parametrization
r:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose support
\gamma\subset\Gamma_{j}
joins the point
x_{1}
with
x_{2}
. Thus,
\gamma
crosses
\partial S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}
and we conclude that
\Gamma_{j}
is connected to
\partial S_{h_{k_{n}},K_{k_{n}}}
.

We claim that
(\widetilde{A}_{n},{\widetilde{S}_{n}})\xrightarrow{\tau_{\mathcal{B}}}(A,S_{h,K})
as
n\to\infty
. In view of (
4.12
), (a2) and the fact that by (
3.7
) and the previous construction of
\widetilde{K}_{n}
,

{\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial\widetilde{S}_{n})=}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial S_{h_{k_{n}},\widetilde{K}_{n}})<\infty,

it remains to prove that

(4.14)

{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial S_{h_{k_{n}},\widetilde{K}_{n}})\to{\rm{sdist}}(\cdot,\partial S_{h,K})

locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
n\to\infty
. Indeed, by Remark
3.8
-(i), it suffices to prove that
S_{h_{k_{n}},\widetilde{K}_{n}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}S_{h}
and that
\Omega\setminus S_{h_{k_{n}},\widetilde{K}_{n}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}\Omega\setminus{\rm{Int}}(S_{h,K})
. On one hand, by the
\tau_{\mathcal{B}}
